# Restart Master Plan — the queue of work, one item at a time

**Date:** 2026-08-26
**Repo:** `Ticketing System Quality Review` (branch `ui-redesign-and-api-hardening`, HEAD `beec159`)
**Source review:** the code-verified audit published 2026-08-26 (artifact "Ticketing Restart Review"). Every gap below was confirmed against code, not against the older planning docs.

> **How to use this file.** This is the *queue*, not an implementation prompt. When an item reaches the top, the planning session writes it up as its own `prompts/YYYY-MM-DD-<item>.md` in the established format (Goal → Context read → Facts established → Decisions → The work → Files → Checks → Manual steps), and an implementer session builds it. Items marked **Needs brainstorming** need a decision from the repo owner before a prompt can be written — the open questions are listed so that conversation is short.
>
> Each item has: **What we are doing** (plain language) · **How** (concrete approach with real file names) · **Size** (S ≤ 2 days, M ≤ 1 week, L 2–3 weeks) · **Depends on** · **Done when**.

---

with a row in the table below has its status **only** there — its `###` section
heading carries no status, because keeping the two in step failed repeatedly (nine
headings went stale, then three more, then cards 1.40 and 1.41 sat five days saying
"handoff written" after they had shipped). **A card with no table row keeps its
status in its heading**, because for those 31 cards the heading is the only record
there is — do not strip it. Every card numbered 1.19 and above lives in the table.

Updated by the planning session as cards move. States: **Queued** → **Handoff written** (prompt exists, kickoff sent) → **Building** (implementer session working) → **Verifying** (planning session re-running checks) → **GREEN** (merge/deploy approved) or **RED** (sent back with reasons) → **Merged** / **Deployed**.

| 0.7 Virus scanning | **✅ DECIDED BY THE OWNER 2026-09-15: NO VIRUS SCANNER. Turn the download gate off instead.** *“option 4 if emails are already verified no need for a scanner”*. **The reasoning, and it is sound for the main case:** attachments arrive through Microsoft 365, where Defender for Office 365 scans them in transit — so an emailed file has already been checked before it reaches the mailbox. A second scan is belt-and-braces. ⚠️ **ACCEPTED RISK, RECORDED SO IT IS NOT REDISCOVERED AS A BUG: files uploaded through the WEB PAGE are not covered by that reasoning.** They go straight from a laptop to Azure Blob with nothing inspecting them. The planner raised this; the owner decided. **Do not re-litigate it — but if the desk ever opens uploads to people outside the organisation, this decision should be revisited.** ✅ **What already exists and is now simply unused:** files go to Azure Blob (`AZURE_STORAGE_CONNECTION_STRING` and `AZURE_STORAGE_CONTAINER` are set in production), there is a `POST /attachments/:id/scan-status` callback waiting to be told CLEAN or INFECTED, and `assertAttachmentDownloadAllowed` reads it. **`INFECTED` still blocks unconditionally even with the gate off** — so if a scanner is ever added, the plumbing is there and nothing needs rebuilding. ⚠️ **THE FLIP IS NOT SAFE ON ITS OWN AND MUST BE SEQUENCED.** Setting `ATTACHMENT_SCAN_ENABLED=false` today would make **card 1.83 live**: a screenshot pasted into an INTERNAL note becomes downloadable by the requester it is about. **The blocked download is the only thing hiding it. 1.83 lands first.** **Order: 1.83 (internal-note files) → 1.105 (stop losing emails) → 3.5 (record who downloaded what) → then set the flag.** Handoff: `prompts/2026-09-15-attachments-and-inbound-batch.md`. | — | Owner (2026-08-26): no decision yet. Production has 0 attachments today, so nothing is stuck. **Must be decided before the first real team uploads a file.** Options on the card; Blob storage is already in place. |

| 1.21 Operations console | **GREEN** (verified 2026-09-01) | `prompts/2026-08-30-1-21-operations-console.md` | Commit `920bc27` (+ baselines in `5127c76`). Covered by the same full re-run as 1.13 (numbers above). `OwnerGuard` confirmed at controller level so it covers **both** routes, with `@ThrottlePolicy('highWrite')` on the POST; integration cases assert 403 for TEAM_ADMIN, LEAD and AGENT, and case 2 asserts the snapshot carries **states only, no secrets or setting values**. The SLA `runOnce()` extraction is behaviour-neutral where it matters: `onModuleInit` **returns before `setInterval` when disabled** (`:64`), so dropping the `enabled` short-circuit from `runOnce()` cannot change the scheduled path — it only means a manual **Run now** on a disabled worker actually runs, which is the point of the console. One integration spec (`sla.instances.spec.ts`) reached the private `checkBreaches()` through a cast and was legitimately repointed at the now-public `runOnce()`. `health.spec.ts` changed by exactly one additive line (`lastSummary: null`) — the `toEqual` assertion was not weakened. | 1.22 Email safety rails | **GREEN** (verified 2026-09-01) — **must be deployed before 1.23 switches sending on** | `prompts/2026-09-01-1-22-email-safety-rails.md` | **Must land before production sends a single email.** Quoted-reply trimming (there is none — verified by grep, so every reply would carry the whole thread), auto-reply/loop protection (none either — nothing checks `Auto-Submitted` or `X-Auto-Response-Suppress`), a per-sender-per-ticket inbound rate cap, bounce suppression, and a **pilot switch** ported from the LMS (`REPORT_TEST_RECIPIENTS`) that reroutes *all* outbound mail to the operator. Code only, no config, no Azure change. Size M. Commit `48c0b74`. Planner re-ran **everything**: api tsc 0, unit **326/38**, full integration **437 passed + 1 skipped, 49 of 50**, web tsc 0 — every figure matches the implementer's report. Confirmed API-only: nothing under `apps/web`, **no migration**, and `/api/health/ready` still reports SMTP `missing`, so this card genuinely sends nothing. Both new vars are in `.env.example`.

**The implementer overrode my §4.4 and was right — and found a live leak doing it.** I specified "throw when an INTERNAL message reaches an outbound payload". That would have **broken working behaviour**: `NotificationsService.messageAdded` deliberately emails internal notes to staff (subject `[Ticket X] Internal note`, body carrying `message.body`). Worse, reading it exposed a real defect: `buildRecipients` adds the requester, then `excludeEmployees` filters on `user.role !== UserRole.EMPLOYEE` — so **a staff member who raises their own ticket is emailed every internal note written on it**, which is ordinary in a helpdesk and is happening today. Their guard is keyed on **`isRequester`, role-agnostic**, thrown from `resolveOutboundRecipients` and called at `email.service.ts:101`, above the single `sendMail` chokepoint. Verified the tests pin all three properties: it throws for the requester; it **still throws when the requester is staff** (the leak); and it **still lets internal notes reach staff who are not the requester** (the feature). My version would have regressed the feature and left the leak open.

Two files outside §6, both disclosed and both correct: `tickets.service.ts` gained `options: { suppressNotifications }` — four additive lines, defaulting to `{}` so no existing caller changes, and **realtime is deliberately not suppressed** because the message still belongs on screen; and `inbound-email.service.spec.ts` for its Prisma mock. **The implementer also caught their own vacuous test:** the first integration spec asserted "no notification raised" on tickets with no assignee and no followers, so it passed whether or not the guard worked. It now assigns each ticket first, so an ordinary reply demonstrably moves the outbox and a suppressed one does not, from identical setup. That catch is worth more than the fix.

**Two errors in my card, both mine:** §2's baselines were three cards stale, and §9 names `test/integration/inbound-email.spec.ts`, which does not exist — the file is `tickets.inbound-email.spec.ts`. |

**Two errors in my card, both mine.** (a) §13 asserted 1.22 was "GREEN **and deployed**" — it is GREEN and *undeployed*. The working recipe is `migrate diff` → hand-write → `migrate deploy`, now in the landmines file.

**Acceptance criterion 3 is unmet by design, and stopping was correct.** The agent-name From line cannot be built from `EmailService` alone: the `MESSAGE_ADDED` outbox payload carries only `{messageId, type}`, so neither the actor nor the team ever reaches the send path — **verified in code**. My §11 named exactly this as a stop condition. Every email currently uses the generic `CSNHC Helpdesk` identity, which is the safe direction to be wrong in. Now **card 1.31**.

The cursor advances **only to a `deltaLink`, never a `nextLink`**, so a restart mid-history cannot strand it. An unknown plus-suffix is ingested **UNROUTED** to the Unassigned queue with a warning naming it — never guessed at, because falling through to whichever team sorts first would put one department's mail in front of another. ⚠️ **My §3 premise was a week stale and they caught it:** I wrote that inbound email does not add the sender as a follower. **Card 1.40 already did**, commit `2c76697` on 2026-09-03 — `inbound-email.service.ts:240`, planner-verified. Chosen over webhooks (push subscriptions expire every few days and silently stop; a fired webhook is lost if the app is down) and over Power Automate (throttling, silent failure, no retry control, production dependency outside the codebase). The delta token is a durable cursor, so a deploy or outage loses nothing. | 1.35 The marker must not eat the inbox preview | **GREEN** — verified 2026-09-02, commit `9638a64` | — briefed inline, no handoff file | **Fixed and independently re-verified.** `insertIntoBody` now places the marker after the opening `<body>` tag, so the document is valid and the hidden preheader leads the inbox preview. **The wrinkle I flagged, I got wrong.** I wrote that putting the preheader *below* the marker "costs nothing since both are at the top". It does not — it restores the exact fault the card exists to fix, because the preview reads document order. The implementer measured five quoting layouts against `stripQuotedReply` instead of taking my word and chose **preheader-first**, which is correct: the trimmer cuts at the marker, so a hidden div above it is discarded with the rest of the quote and never reaches the agent's view. The measurement is recorded in a comment at the function, which is the right place for it.

**Not yet in production.** Committed after the deploy zip was built — I checked the 13:53 package for `insertIntoBody` and it is absent, so the batch could not have been contaminated. Ships with the next deploy. Until then the live preview still leads with the marker; that is expected, not a regression. | `EmailService.decorateHtmlBody` prepends `<p>marker</p>` to the **entire document**, producing `<p>…</p><!DOCTYPE html><html>…`. Two faults: the document is **malformed** — content before the doctype drops clients into quirks mode and leaves the marker outside `<html>`, where Outlook's rendering is least predictable — and **the marker leads the inbox preview instead of the preheader**, which is confirmed rather than theoretical (the pre-1.34 preview read "----- Reply above this line ----- Update on your request Hello…"). Card 1.34 made the preview carry the agent's question, but the marker still takes ~33 of the ~90 characters that decide whether the email is opened at all. **The fix:** insert the marker **after the opening `<body>` tag** rather than before the doctype — valid document, preheader first. **The wrinkle to think about before coding:** the preheader would then sit *above* the marker, and `stripQuotedReply` cuts at the marker — so a hidden div could survive trimming and land in the agent's view of the requester's reply. Check what the trimmer actually leaves behind; the answer may be to put the preheader below the marker instead, which costs nothing since both are at the top. Pre-existing (1.22's prepending), API only, no migration. Size S. |

| 1.38 A public reply is saved as an internal note | **GREEN** — verified 2026-09-03, re-ran everything myself: api `tsc` 0, unit **443/44**, integration **475 + 1 skipped, 53 of 54**, web `tsc` 0, vitest **100/21**; no schema, migration or lockfile change, 52 migrations. Commits `48d9874` / `e6cbf7f` / `c2b8584` (stage 2); was live, cost nothing — see below; sensitive | `prompts/2026-09-02-1-38-public-reply-saved-as-internal.md` | **On an unassigned ticket an AGENT cannot post a public reply at all, and is told "Reply sent".** Found doing the browser pass for 1.37 — which is the whole argument for doing browser passes. I posted through the real composer with the toggle plainly reading `Public`; it stored `INTERNAL`, with **zero outbox rows**. Then bypassed the UI: `curl -d '{"type":"PUBLIC"}'` → `{"type":"INTERNAL"}`, **201**. Not a UI state bug — the server overrides and reports success.

**Cause — two files hold opposite beliefs about one case.** API `access-control.service.ts:215` asks `assigneeId === user.id`; on an unassigned ticket `null` never equals a user id, so it falls through and **every AGENT on the team is a "peer agent" on every unassigned ticket**. Web `TicketDetailPage.tsx:383` returns early on `!ticket.assignee` — *"unassigned tickets are open to any agent"*. The API then overrides at `tickets.service.ts:1524` behind a comment reading *"the UI also hides the toggle, but defense-in-depth"* — **which is false in exactly this case**, so the "defense" is the only thing deciding the outcome.

**And the screen keeps lying afterwards.** The client has the true type and uses it in one place of three: `:1460` uses `serverMessage.type` for the realtime event, but `:1452` updates only `localStatus` and never the type, and `:1467` keys the toast off local intent → **"Reply sent"**. With 1.37 on top of it there is no signal anywhere on the screen that the message stayed private.

**Settled 2026-09-03 by two read-only production queries — and I reached the right answer twice with the wrong reasoning in between.** The facts: production has **exactly one AGENT, `phulgur@csnhc.com`, active** (the owner's own account) and **75** unassigned tickets, not 53. So 1.38 **was live in principle**. But it caused **no damage**: `silent-internal-check.mjs` found **0** internal messages written by an agent on a ticket they were not assigned, so **no requester is waiting for a reply that was silently kept private.** And `PA_20260902_046` **is assigned to that agent**, so `isPeerAgent` returned false and **1.38 never applied to the incident — card 1.37 explains that night**, exactly as this card originally said.

**My reasoning, for the record, because it went wrong in a way worth not repeating.** I first said 1.38 probably did not explain the incident — right conclusion, wrong reason: I claimed the account was TEAM_ADMIN, which I had invented, most likely by reading dev Supabase where it is EMPLOYEE. When the query showed AGENT I reversed to "it probably does" — wrong, because I still had not checked the one field that decides it, the ticket's **assignee**. The rule is `assigneeId === user.id`, so role was never sufficient on its own. **Check the field the code actually reads, not a proxy for it.** One residual caveat, stated rather than buried: both queries use the assignee as it stands **now**, so a ticket assigned *after* those messages were posted would not show up. It does not change the outcome here — the three messages on that ticket were `"This is an agent message, should be sent as an email"`, `"now ?"` and `"Test 4"`, which are tests, not answers anyone is owed.

**OWNER DECIDED 2026-09-02: an agent must assign a ticket to themselves before replying to the requester** — replies stay tied to an owner. So **the API is the authority and needs no change**; the web must stop offering `Public` where the server will refuse it, and must name the way out ("assign this to yourself to reply"), which the sidebar's **Me** button already provides. A side effect worth noting: the comment at `tickets.service.ts:1524` claiming *"the UI also hides the toggle"* **becomes true**. Applies to role `AGENT` only — LEAD, TEAM_ADMIN and OWNER keep replying publicly on unassigned tickets. **Now combined with 1.37** into `prompts/2026-09-02-1-37-and-1-38-combined.md`, since both touch the same two files and either alone leaves the confusion half-solved. **Web only.** I had recommended the opposite (let agents reply, change the API); the owner's rule is accountability and it makes the code honest instead of the comment wrong. Superseded note (§5): *should an AGENT be able to publicly reply to an unassigned ticket?* **My recommendation — yes, change the API to match the web.** The rule exists to stop an agent talking to a requester on *someone else's* ticket; an unassigned ticket is nobody's, and the opposite rule makes the queue unworkable, since the first agent to pick up a new ticket could not answer it without assigning it to themselves first. **Task 1 is worth landing either way** — the client must believe the server's type, not its own intent. **This is the second instance of 1.36 Fault C's shape**, so it is worth writing down as a pattern: a permission question answered independently in two layers will drift, and the UI's answer is the one the user sees. Web + a one-line API decision, no migration. Size S. |
| 1.37 An agent cannot see what they sent | **GREEN** — verified 2026-09-03, re-ran everything myself: api `tsc` 0, unit **443/44**, integration **475 + 1 skipped, 53 of 54**, web `tsc` 0, vitest **100/21**; no schema, migration or lockfile change, 52 migrations. Three of `phulgur@`'s messages on `PA_20260902_046` were `INTERNAL`; the author saw ordinary blue "sent" bubbles and concluded the email pipeline was broken. **Nothing was broken** — 1.33 correctly sends no email for an internal note.

**Both faults now confirmed in the browser as well as in code** (2026-09-02, dev ticket `IS_20260609_002`, screenshots in the session scratchpad). The two views side by side: as the **author**, six internal messages all render in the ordinary blue "sent" bubble with a badge on one of them; as a **different viewer**, the same six render amber under a **single** `Internal` badge — which is Fault B at its worst, six internal messages sharing one marker. The author's view also confirmed §4.3's claim that `PUBLIC` is the correct non-sticky mount default: a fresh page load came up on `Public`.

**Fault A**, `TicketConversation.tsx:341` — verified: `isCurrentUser ? primary : isInternal ? amber : card`. `isCurrentUser` short-circuits, so the amber treatment is unreachable for your own notes and works only for *other people's*, which is the case needing it least. The card is right that reordering the ternary is the wrong fix — it would cost the left/right sent/received distinction the layout depends on.

**Fault B**, same file `:298` — verified: the `Internal` badge sits inside an `isGroupStart ?` header, so only the first message of a run carries it. The card's supporting observation checks out too: the grouping predicate at `:251-255` includes `previousMessage.type === message.type`, so **a group is never mixed** and the badge is safe to render per-message. **Fault C** (the composer stays on Internal for the session) is contributing and lower severity; the reset-after-post option is the owner's call.

**I checked for a second renderer before agreeing** — line 236's comment says "Mirrors ConversationPane", and I have previously fixed an orphaned component instead of the live one. `ConversationPane` **does not exist**; the comment references a deleted file. `TicketConversation.tsx` is reached only from `TicketDetailPage.tsx`, so there is one copy to fix and a stale comment worth deleting while in there.

**Why this outranks 1.36:** today's failure was harmless — expected public, got internal, no email sent. **The mirror image is the risk:** an agent believes the toggle is on Internal, writes something candid about a requester, and since 1.23 that message really does leave the building. Same blind spot, opposite direction. **Design it with 1.28** — both are "the audience for this message is invisible"; they can ship apart. **One correction:** the card's API baseline of 405 unit is stale, it is **416/43** after 1.35. (Its `AGENT` label for `phulgur@` was **right** — see the 1.36 row for my false "correction" of it.) Web only, no API change, no migration. Size S. |
| 1.36 A staff member's own ticket | **GREEN** — verified 2026-09-03, re-ran everything myself: api `tsc` 0, unit **443/44**, integration **475 + 1 skipped, 53 of 54**, web `tsc` 0, vitest **100/21**; no schema, migration or lockfile change, 52 migrations. Commits `48d9874` / `e6cbf7f` / `c2b8584` (stage 1); sensitive | `prompts/2026-09-02-1-36-a-staff-members-own-ticket.md` | **Two faults, one cause: the app decides what someone may see on a ticket from their *rank*, never asking whether they *raised it*.** Same root cause as the hole card 1.22 closed on the send path — that one was about email, these are in-app.

**Fault A — a staff requester reads the internal notes about themselves.** Verified at `tickets.service.ts:1026`: the message-list query is `...(user.role === UserRole.EMPLOYEE ? { type: MessageType.PUBLIC } : {})`. The only question asked is *are you an EMPLOYEE?* So a LEAD, TEAM_ADMIN or OWNER who is the **requester** on a ticket sees every `INTERNAL` note on it — including notes written about them. The scenario is concrete: payroll is the only department operationally receiving tickets, so a payroll lead with a problem about her own pay has nowhere else to file it, and "agents will not raise tickets to their own department" is not available as a mitigation. It never applied to the three OWNER accounts anyway, whose `roleFilter` returns `{}`.

**Fault B — a staff member cannot see their own ticket at all.** Verified in `access-control.service.ts` `roleFilter`: EMPLOYEE gets `{ requesterId: user.id }`, but **LEAD and TEAM_ADMIN get team scope with no requester clause**. So staff raising a ticket to a team they are not on cannot see it — not in a list, not by URL, no reply, no confirmation it was resolved. Note the near-miss at `:59-61`: someone with **no** team falls back to `requesterId`, so the fault bites precisely the staff who *do* have a team, which is all of them.

**Latent, not live:** all 46 production tickets came from floor staff through the Power Automate intake and **zero** were raised by a staff account. Both faults become real on the same ticket the moment one is. **Fix:** add "or is the requester" to the message filter, and "or requested by me" to `roleFilter` — rank still governs everything else. The card's own warning is the right instinct: **check every sibling read** (single-message fetch, search, export, the AI context builder) or the next one becomes the hole.

**⚠️ I "corrected" this card's table and I was wrong.** I wrote that it mislabels `phulgur@` as AGENT and that production has TEAM_ADMIN. **Production has exactly one AGENT and it is `phulgur@csnhc.com`, active** — confirmed 2026-09-03 by a read-only query against `csh-ticketing-db` (EMPLOYEE 53, AGENT 1, LEAD 1, TEAM_ADMIN 1, OWNER 3). The card was right; my correction was not, and I repeated it on three rows. It most likely came from reading a different database — dev Supabase has `phulgur@` as EMPLOYEE, so this account has three different roles across three environments, which is exactly the confusion card 1.30 exists to clear up. **Consequence: the AGENT-only faults apply to the owner's own account** — which is why 1.38 was live at all. See the 1.38 row for what that did and did not cost. **A third fault was added to the card after I boarded it, and it is the only one of the three that has actually cost anyone time — hit in production today.** **Fault C: the web and the API disagree about what "on the team" means.** Verified in both layers. `auth.guard.ts:118` resolves `membership?.teamId ?? user.primaryTeamId ?? null`, and `operationalTeamIds` then falls back to that — so the API accepts `primaryTeamId` as team scope. `TicketDetailPage.tsx:334` computes `teamMembers.some(m => m.user.email === currentEmail)`, roster rows **only**, and `canManage` gates the assign control on it. So `phulgur@` could open payroll tickets but the assign control never rendered, and **no request ever reached the server to be refused** — the most confusing shape a permission bug can take. `canAssignTicket` would have allowed it on any of the 53 unassigned tickets. Resolved live by adding the roster row.

**One correction to the card's framing:** it presents this as two layers disagreeing. It is actually a **three-tier fallback** — `memberTeamIds` → `teamId` → `primaryTeamId` — of which the web implements only the first tier. That matters for the fix: mirroring one fallback in the web still leaves the other. The API is also internally inconsistent, since `memberTeamIds` is built from roster rows with no fallback while `teamId` has one. **Pick one definition and apply it in all three places**, and make a mismatched account fail loudly rather than silently — the card's instinct here is right.

**Sensitivity:** the card describes a live weakness and both GitHub remotes are public; handle as `docs/security-audit-2026-08.md`. A and B are API-only; C is one line of web against one line of API. No migration. Size S. |
| 1.28 The agent can see and control who an email reaches | **GREEN** — verified 2026-09-03, re-ran everything myself: api `tsc` 0, unit **443/44**, integration **475 + 1 skipped, 53 of 54**, web `tsc` 0, vitest **100/21**; no schema, migration or lockfile change, 52 migrations. Commits `48d9874` / `e6cbf7f` / `c2b8584` (stage 3) (2026-09-02) — **next up after 1.33 + 1.34 deploy** | `prompts/2026-09-02-1-28-who-this-reaches.md` | Part of the email epic, and the piece the original 1.22–1.25 scope missed. Once looped-in people stay on the thread, **every later agent reply also reaches them** — HR loops in a payroll manager about a termination, and two days later a more candid message goes to them too. So: the participant list must be **visible directly above the compose box**, not buried in settings, with the ability to remove someone **before** sending; and each posted message must show what actually happened to it ("emailed to 3 people" / "internal — not sent") so an agent knows what the requester has seen. **Dependency corrected 2026-09-02: this now depends on 1.33, not 1.24, and it became more urgent rather than less.** 1.33 makes a public reply **one email with a CC list**, so from the moment 1.33 ships an agent types into the chat and it silently reaches several people — and **the agent cannot see who**. On a payroll or termination ticket that is a safety gap, not a UX nicety: someone writes candidly without realising three colleagues are CC'd. Before 1.33 the risk was theoretical because nobody was looped in yet; after it, the participant list is the thing standing between an agent and an unintended audience. **Do this immediately after 1.33 deploys.** Size M. |

| 1.46 Tag creation raced under bulk | **GREEN** — found and fixed inside 1.12, commit `9b6c540` | — | **No work left; recorded because the reasoning is worth keeping.** `TagsService` created tags with `tag.upsert`, which Prisma compiles to a read then a write: two requests creating the same new tag both miss the read, both insert, one loses on `Tag.name`'s unique index. Harmless while every caller was single-ticket; card 1.12 tags up to a hundred, so **bulk-tagging with a new tag failed on most of the selection.** The fix that looks right and is not: catching `P2002` and re-reading — the macro executor runs **inside a transaction**, so the violation aborts it and every later statement answers `25P02 current transaction is aborted`, the recovery read included. Fixed with `INSERT … ON CONFLICT ("name") DO NOTHING` so the conflict is avoided rather than survived. |
| 1.47 Redaction can be outrun by a pending email | **GREEN** — verified 2026-09-09, commit `842e87a` (+ `0c69e4c`). `cancelUnsentForRedaction` is a per-row `updateMany` conditional on `status: PENDING` that returns the rows it won — the race-safe shape the card required. Scrub covers **SENT and FAILED** (the browser pass found the FAILED gap) and also blanks `payload.content.html`, **a second copy of the text the planner never flagged**; blanking `body` alone would have left it. Reuses `FAILED` rather than adding an enum value, so no migration. | — | **Redact a message within about a minute of posting it — exactly when you would notice — and the dialog tells you nothing was emailed, then the original text is emailed anyway.** Every link verified in code: **(1)** posting a public reply writes a `NotificationOutbox` row whose `body` is the rendered email containing `message.body` (`notifications.service.ts:998`), status `PENDING`. **(2)** Production has **no Redis app setting**, so the BullMQ queue cannot deliver; what actually delivers is `EmailOutboxSweeperService`, a `setInterval` whose default is **60_000 ms** with `EMAIL_OUTBOX_SWEEP_INTERVAL_MS` unset in production (`email-outbox-sweeper.service.ts:19`). **So `PENDING` lasts up to a minute, not an instant.** **(3)** `redactMessage` computes its caveat from `messageDeliveryLabels`, which counts **only `SENT`** rows (`tickets.service.ts:1215`), so mid-window `emailed = 0` and **no "already emailed" warning is shown at all.** **(4)** `redactMessage` overwrites `TicketMessage.body` and **never touches the outbox row.** **(5)** The sweeper then claims that row and `email-processor.service.ts:79` sends `text: record.body` — **the stored, unredacted text**, read from the database at send time. ⚠️ **The code comment at `tickets.service.ts:1187` says "the send runs at queue time, so PENDING is momentary." That is not true in production.** **The fix has two halves and the first is the urgent one:** on redaction, **cancel or blank any still-`PENDING` outbox row for that message** — redact fast enough and the email should never leave — and make the caveat distinguish *already sent* from *about to be sent*, which is a different sentence and a different decision for the agent. Second, the original half of this card: a `SENT` row keeps the full rendered body in `NotificationOutbox.body` **for ever**, because the retention job that would delete it is off (confirmed against production 2026-09-08). Card 1.11's own argument — that preserving redacted text moves PHI into a row with weaker read rules — applies to that column exactly as it did to the `TicketEvent` the implementer refused to write. ✅ **CORRECTED 2026-09-09 against production settings and rows — the planner and the implementer were each half wrong, and the real numbers are below.** **(a) My 60-second PENDING window was wrong.** `NOTIFICATIONS_QUEUE_ENABLED = false` in production, so `EmailQueueService` processes **inline, in the same request** — it branches on that flag alone, not on Redis being reachable. My "no Redis, therefore the 60-second sweeper delivers" reasoning does not apply. **The implementer caught this and was right.** With inline sending there is **no window at all**: the mail is gone before an agent could reach the Remove control, so the cancel-a-pending-send half is **dormant as configured today** — correct to build, because it engages the moment the queue is switched on, but not a live hole. **(b) The implementer's "production has no SMTP, so every message email is FAILED" is also wrong — it is true only historically.** Production **is** configured today: `SMTP_HOST = smtp.socketlabs.com`, port 587. Of the `MESSAGE_ADDED` rows, **12 are SENT and 8 FAILED**, and the FAILED ones all read *"SMTP not configured"* — legacy from before the setting existed. So mail really does leave production now. **(c) The stale-body half is the live problem, and it is measurable: 274 of 274 SENT outbox rows still hold a non-empty body**, with the retention job off. Add the 8 FAILED message rows the implementer found keeping their text too. **That is the part to fix for real.** **Does this block the 1.11 deploy?** Planner's read: **no** — without redaction the email goes out regardless, so this is not a regression, and real requester email traffic is still minimal. But it **misreports**, and false reassurance is the part that matters: an agent who redacts inside the window will believe the words never left the building. **Fix it before redaction is described to staff.** |

Three filters, one of which the planner had not thought of: ids are read out of **agent-authored HTML**, so an id naming another ticket's file must not be actionable. Also skips any file another live message still references, so copied markup does not break. The blob is deleted **after** the transaction commits — deleting inside it and rolling back would lose a file from a message that was never redacted — and logs rather than throws. Worth answering before the feature is described to staff as "remove a message that should not have been sent", because that is what they will reach for. **S–M.** |
| 1.49 The ticket-row checkbox does nothing | **GREEN** — verified 2026-09-09, commit `a513499` (+ `0c69e4c`). `event.target !== event.currentTarget` on the row's `onKeyDown`, so a key from the checkbox no longer both ticks the box and navigates away. ⚠️ **The first fix made it worse than the dead control** — Space selected and then abandoned the list — and the implementer found that in the browser, not the suite. Wiring extracted into a pure function **because this project's vitest has no jsdom**, which is what made it assertable at all. | — | Clicking the checkbox in a ticket row does not select it. **Exact mechanism, `TicketTableView.tsx:212-229`:** the `<td>` at `:213` owns the toggle (`stopPropagation` + `preventDefault` + `selection.toggle`), while the `<input>` at :221 has `onChange={() => {}}` — **a no-op** — and `onClick={(e) => e.stopPropagation()}`. So a click on the box is stopped before it reaches the `<td>`, and the only handler that could act is empty. Clicking the **padding** around the box works, which is why it reads as a flaky UI rather than a dead control. ⚠️ **It is also a keyboard/accessibility bug, which nobody has mentioned:** Space on a focused checkbox dispatches a click, so it dies the same way — the control is unreachable by keyboard entirely, and the row has no other way to be selected. ⚠️ **Why the suite is green on it:** the one test that touches this (`ticket-table-awaiting-reply.test.tsx:43`) passes `toggle: () => {}` as a stub and uses the checkbox's `aria-label` only as a **text anchor** to find a row. **Nothing anywhere clicks the checkbox and asserts the selection changed.** Any fix must add that test, or the next refactor breaks it again invisibly. **The fix:** give the `onChange` the toggle and leave the `<input>`'s `stopPropagation` in place so the row-click does not also fire; the `<td>` may keep toggling for the bigger click target. ⚠️ **Do not end up with both the input's `onChange` and the `<td>`'s `onClick` firing for one click** — two toggles net to zero and it looks identical to this bug. Not caused by card 1.12, but it matters more now that 1.12 has made bulk selection something agents will use every day. **XS.** |

| 1.51 The bulk macro times out on a slow link | **GREEN** — verified 2026-09-09, commit `dd6c4bb` (+ `0c69e4c`). `MACRO_TRANSACTION_OPTIONS = { timeout: 15_000 }` — the planner's recommendation, not the scheduler's 60 s, which would hold locks under bulk. The SLA sync stays **inside** the transaction, so a rolled-back priority change cannot leave an inconsistent SLA row. And the duplicate `BULK_CONCURRENCY` was **deleted** rather than kept in step; it had been dead since card 1.12. | — | A bulk macro across three tickets failed on one with a Prisma interactive-transaction timeout: **5000 ms allowed, 5037 ms elapsed**, inside `slaInstance.upsert`. Confirmed in code, all three links: `rule-engine.service.ts:499` opens the macro transaction with **no `timeout` option**, so it takes Prisma's 5000 ms default; `:667` calls `slaEngine.syncFromTicket(…, tx)` **inside** that transaction on the `set_priority` action; and that reaches `sla-engine.service.ts:107` `slaInstance.upsert`. With `BULK_CONCURRENCY = 5` against a remote pooler, five of those run at once. **Latency, not logic** — the retry succeeded immediately. **What worked is worth keeping:** the rollback was clean (that ticket kept SEV3 and got no tag, no half-applied state) and the per-ticket report named the ticket and the reason, which is exactly the argument for per-ticket over all-or-nothing. **The integration suite cannot see this** — local Postgres is far faster than the remote pooler. ⚠️ **Planner's recommendation: raise the timeout, do not move the SLA sync out of the transaction.** Moving it out would let the SLA row survive a priority change that rolled back, trading a visible timeout for silent inconsistency. There is precedent for an override at `automation-scheduler.service.ts:348` (`{ timeout: 60_000, maxWait: 10_000 }`), but **60 s is too long here** — a long-held transaction under bulk starves a pooled connection. Something like 15 s, with concurrency left at 5, bounds the tail without holding locks. ⚠️ **And whoever takes this must know `BULK_CONCURRENCY` is written twice** — `run-bulk-with-concurrency.util.ts:2` **and** `tickets.service.ts:2843` — so tuning one leaves the other behind. That is the same one-rule-in-two-places drift as cards 1.36, 1.38 and 1.50. **S.** |

| 1.53 Team admin manages saved views for the team | **GREEN** — verified 2026-09-10, commit `5b22bf4`. **Migration 59** — one additive `TEXT[]` column with an empty default, **0 DROPs**, hand-checked. Share-with-my-team is a checkbox in the existing Save-view popover, **hidden rather than disabled** for those who may not use it; shared rows carry a **Team badge**, because deleting a view you think is yours is the obvious hazard; hiding is ten tick-boxes on the Team page. Browser-verified across three accounts: an IT team admin created a team view and hid *SEV1 today*; an IT agent saw the view, the badge and a live count of 24 and no SEV1; an HR account saw neither change. On a merge the surviving team's hidden list wins and the source's is announced with `RAISE NOTICE`, because hiding something people rely on is worse than showing something they had turned off. ⚠️ **One instruction of mine was impossible — see card 1.60.** | — | **Requirement (owner, verbatim intent):** keep per-user saved views exactly as they are, and **additionally** let a **team admin create, modify and delete saved views visible to the whole team**. This is mostly permissions and UI.** **❌ What is missing, verified:** **(1) There is no role gate at all** — `create()` honours `dto.teamId` whenever it equals the caller's own team (`:30-33`), with **no role check**, so today *any* user, an EMPLOYEE included, can create a team-wide view. The requirement makes it **stricter**, not looser. **(2)** `update()` and `delete()` gate only on `existing.userId !== user.id` (`:60`, `:88`), so a team view can be edited by **its creator alone** — **another team admin cannot touch it, and it is orphaned the day that person leaves.** **(3)** `update()` cannot change `teamId` at all, so there is no way to promote a personal view to a team view or demote one back. **(4)** No UI to create or manage a team view, and the sidebar draws no distinction between *my* view and *the team's* view — people need to know which they are about to edit. ⚠️ **(5) A live bug this will make worse:** both `create()` and `update()` clear the default flag with `updateMany({ where: { userId: user.id } })` (`:37-40`, `:66-69`), which spans **both kinds** — so making a report view the default already clears the default *ticket* view, and vice versa. Team views turn one shared flag into a three-way fight. **Decide what `isDefault` even means on a team view before writing any of this.** ⚠️ **The harder half, and the owner's actual pain:** the ten sidebar entries in the screenshot are **hard-coded constants**, not saved views — `SAVED_VIEWS` in `components/shell/saved-views.ts`, each with its own `buildQuery()` and `matches()`. They are shown to **everyone** and cannot be hidden. So simply *adding* team views leaves payroll looking at ten irrelevant rows above their four, which is not what was asked for. ✅ **DECIDED by the owner 2026-09-09: hide the built-ins, and only a team admin controls it.** Not "replace when team views exist" — explicit hiding, team-admin-gated. So the presets keep their tested `matches()` active-state logic and their count wiring; a team simply stops rendering the ones it has hidden. **Consequences to build to, all settled by that decision:** **(i) Storage:** `Team` has **no settings JSON** and the schema's house style is explicit columns with doc comments (`isSensitive`, `confidenceThreshold`), so add **`hiddenPresetIds String[]` to `Team`** — one additive column, empty by default. **This is migration 59 and it must land AFTER the 58 deploy**, never beside it. **(ii) Only a team admin controls it means members cannot opt back in.** If payroll's admin hides *SEV1 today*, an AGENT on payroll cannot get it back. **That is what was asked for — build it, but do not also add a personal override**, because two mechanisms answering "is this row visible" is the drift that produced 1.36, 1.38, 1.47 and 1.50. **OWNER manages any team's list; TEAM_ADMIN manages their own team's.** **(iii) A preset id is a code constant, not a row.** A hidden id whose preset is later renamed or deleted must be ignored silently, never rendered as a ghost and never a crash — and `SAVED_VIEWS` ids are the contract, so **treat them as stable and say so in that file's doc comment.** **(iv) Multi-team people:** `AuthUser` carries both a resolved single `teamId` (`auth.guard.ts:140`) **and** `memberTeamIds`, the full set (`:147`), and `SavedViewsService.list()` uses **only** the single `teamId` today. **Use the primary `teamId` for the hidden list too**, matching what team views already do — unioning hidden ids across several teams hides too much, intersecting hides nothing, and neither is what anyone means. `memberTeamIds` is there if the owner ever wants multi-team. **Say in the report that a multi-team member sees only their primary team's views and hides.** **(v) `isDefault` on a team view: planner's call — don't allow it.** Keep the flag strictly personal, which sidesteps the three-way fight entirely; a team admin who wants everyone to *land* somewhere is asking for a team landing view, which nobody has requested. **And fix the existing bug while you are in there:** scope the default-clearing `updateMany` to the same `viewType`, so a default report view stops clearing a default ticket view. **Note:** *TCA Urgent Retro / A-PAF* style views are presumably tag- or category-filtered. A saved view stores `filters` JSON and the API has always accepted `tags?: string[]` (`list-tickets.dto.ts:151`), so **this does not depend on card 1.50** — the broken control there is the input box, not the filter. **Counts — owner asked, 2026-09-09: yes, and team views get them for free.** `useViewCounts` (`shell/use-view-count.ts`) issues **one real `GET /tickets` per view** with `pageSize=1` and reads `meta.total`, and `viewFiltersToParams` already converts a stored `SavedView.filters` blob into those params — **it does not care whose view it is**, so a team view counts with no extra work. Three honest qualifications: **(a)** it is a 60-second cached fetch (`staleTime: 60_000`) with `placeholderData` holding the previous number, **not** a push subscription — so a badge can be up to a minute stale and refreshes on remount or refocus, not when a ticket changes; **(b)** every badge is **its own COUNT query**, so ten presets plus four team views is fourteen filtered counts per sidebar load against the remote pooler — the same pooler that produced card 1.51's 5-second transaction timeout. **This is a second, independent argument for letting a team hide the presets it does not use: payroll would go from fourteen count queries to four.** **(c)** The count runs through `/tickets`, which applies the viewer's own access filter — so **one team view shows different numbers to different members**, and an EMPLOYEE sees only what they may open. That is correct and should stay, but it means a team count is not a team-wide total and must not be described as one. **Also fix in passing:** the doc comment atop `shell/saved-views.ts` says the presets feed `/tickets-revamp`, a page deleted 2026-06-08. **M.** |
| 1.54 An expired session says "Unable to load tickets" | **GREEN** — verified 2026-09-09, commit `86537c3`. Planner re-ran everything. **The card's real deliverable came back and it reframes the problem:** of twenty `UnauthorizedException` paths, production could only ever have produced about **four** — `validateRegisteredClaims` runs on the **HS256** branch only, while production is Azure/RS256, where a bare `catch` collapsed expiry, a forged signature, a wrong audience and unreachable JWKS into one `Invalid bearer token`. `joseRejectionDetail` now lifts jose's own codes into the log while the HTTP response stays vague. The burst was **16 × `Unknown user`** and one genuine expiry. **Both §1c leads were real**, and a third the planner missed: the cacheable-GET path called `onAuthFailure()` directly, bypassing the latch, so ten sidebar counts could fire ten `loginRedirect`s — the exact storm the latch exists to prevent. The 46 ms gap was **not** a retry: `acquireTokenSilent`'s `catch { return null }` means no second request is ever made. | — | **What the owner sees:** the ticket list shows *"Unable to load tickets"* with a Retry button, while the sidebar still shows plausible counts — so it reads as one broken query. **It is not. Every request on the page had failed.** **Evidence, from the production container log for 2026-09-09:** **eleven `401`s in a single 70 ms burst at 13:36:30.954–31.021** — the ten sidebar count queries plus `/api/tickets?page=1&pageSize=50&scope=assigned&sort=updatedAt&order=desc&statusGroup=open` **twice**, which is exactly *Assigned to Me + Open*, the screen in the owner's screenshot. Across the whole day: **1030 × 200, 227 × 304, 11 × 401, 5 × 201 — zero 5xx and zero 429.** So this is **not** a server fault, not the database, and not rate limiting. `pid 1867` is constant all day, so **the container never restarted** either — it is not a cold JWKS cache. **Why it looks like only the list broke:** `useViewCounts` sets `placeholderData: (prev) => prev` (`shell/use-view-count.ts`), so each badge keeps its **last good number on screen** when a refetch fails. The counts the owner saw were stale. The list has no placeholder, so only it showed the error. **⚠️ The fix that matters most, and it is small: the API never logs WHY it rejected a token.** `auth.guard.ts` has **twenty distinct `UnauthorizedException` paths** — expired, not-yet-active, bad issuer, bad audience, bad signature, unsupported alg, missing email claim, unknown user, JWKS misconfigured — and the log records only `statusCode: 401`. **Nothing in the log distinguishes them, which is why a bug the owner hits frequently cannot be diagnosed.** Log the reason (the exception message and the offending claim, **never the token**). Do this first; it makes the rest of this card answerable instead of speculative. **Second: an auth failure must not be dressed as a data-loading failure.** *"Unable to load tickets"* plus Retry is the wrong surface — Retry cannot fix an expired session, so the owner clicks it and it fails again, which is exactly what "frequently" feels like. It should say the session expired and re-authenticate. **Third, and unverified — do not treat this part as established:** the client already refreshes and retries once, then calls `fireAuthFailure()` → `loginRedirect` (`client.ts:583-620`, `useAuthSession.ts:575-597`). Two things look wrong. **(a)** The two list 401s are **46 ms apart**, far too fast for the network round trip `acquireTokenSilent({ forceRefresh: true })` needs — so that retry is most likely **TanStack Query's own retry**, and the silent refresh failed immediately rather than being attempted properly. **(b)** `fireAuthFailure` is latched by a **module-level `authFailureFired` flag** reset only by `setOnAuthFailure`, so after the first auth failure in a page session **the re-login redirect can never fire again** — which would leave exactly this dead error panel. **Confirm both against a real expired session before changing them.** **S–M.** |

Do not leave it indefinitely. **The three places, and only these three** — checked, the value is nowhere in the repo and not in any local `.env`; only the *name* appears in code and `.env.example`: **(1)** the **`INTAKE_API_SECRET` app setting** on `TicketTicket` / `csnhc-ai` — what the API checks against. **(2)** the **Power Automate flow's `x-intake-secret` header** — what sends it. **(3)** `C:\Users\PHulgur\ticketticket-intake-secret.txt` — 49 bytes of plaintext in the owner's home folder since 2026-08-28. **Delete that one rather than updating it.** ⚠️ **(1) and (2) must happen together.** The comparison is against a single secret with `timingSafeEqual` (`intake.service.ts:61-72`) — **there is no two-secret overlap**, so from the moment the app setting changes until the flow has the new value, **form submissions are refused with 403.** Have the flow open before starting, and submit one test form afterwards to confirm. ⚠️ Changing an app setting **restarts the App Service**, so expect a cold start on the first request. If a deploy is happening anyway, doing both in one window costs one restart instead of two. **The script does step (1) only** and prints just the first four characters, writing the value to a file rather than the terminal. | 1.66 A trimmed reply can truncate an agent's own note | **GREEN** — verified 2026-09-11, commit `5e14df4`. Two markers removed, not six — `/^_{5,}\s*$/m` and `/^--[ \t]?$/m`. **The argument is closed rather than probabilistic, and I checked it:** the cut is made at the EARLIEST match, so removing a marker can only ever trim LESS, never more — the failure direction is showing an agent some quoted text, never eating their words. The four survivors are unambiguous mail artefacts. ✅ **They found a third failing test I had not predicted and the fix was better than deleting it:** `email.service.spec.ts`'s “Outlook rule” case used a bare underscore rule with NO header block, which real Outlook never sends — they made the fixture realistic (rule + `From:`/`Sent:`) so the client shape the case is named for still trims, via marker 3. **Non-vacuous by construction:** the new agent-note test contains both removed markers, so restoring either cuts it at the divider and the test fails. | — | **Card 1.62 wired `stripQuotedReply` into `listMessages` (`tickets.service.ts:1203`), the single message-read path — so it now runs on every displayed message, including ones an agent typed.** Two of its six markers are things a person might write: `/^_{5,}\s*$/m` (five or more underscores as a divider) and `/^--[ \t]?$/m` (a bare `--`). An agent using either would see their note **silently truncated on screen**. The stored body is intact, so this is display-only — but an agent seeing their own note cut short is bad enough. **The implementer's read was that fixing it properly needs a way to tell agent-authored from inbound, which needs a migration** — `TicketMessage` has only `authorId` and `type`, no origin column, and `InboundEmailReceipt.messageId` is the RFC id rather than a foreign key. **That is all true. But there is a smaller fix.** ✅ **Planner's recommendation: delete markers 5 and 6 from the display trim. No migration, no origin column, no behaviour lost.** The argument, and it is complete: `stripQuotedReply` cuts at the **earliest** match across all six markers, so markers 5 and 6 can only change the outcome when they match **earlier than** markers 1–4 or when **none of 1–4 match at all**. Markers 1–4 — our own `----- Reply above this line -----`, `-----Original Message-----`, `From:` followed by `Sent:`/`Date:`, and `On … wrote:` — are unambiguous email artefacts that a genuine reply essentially always carries. **So markers 5 and 6 only ever bite a message with no email signal whatsoever, which is precisely an agent's note.** **Checked against the real message** in `__fixtures__/outlook-reply.html`: markers 5 and 6 match **zero** times; the trimming is done by markers 1 and 3. **The two risky markers contribute nothing to the case the card was built for.** Worst case from dropping them is cosmetic — on an Outlook forward the cut lands at the `From:` line instead of the horizontal rule one line above it. **XS.** |

| 1.69 ⚠️ The rate limit is keyed on a constant IP | **GREEN** — verified 2026-09-10, commit `e2e0d76`. **Steps 2, 3 and 4 done** (`e2e0d76`, `b45c4da`, `9bf4af9`). **Step 1 — raising `RATE_LIMIT_LIMIT` — is the owner's, still unset in production, and mitigation only.** ✅ **Step 2's probe is the deliverable I most wanted and it proved my trap real:** `{ hasUser: false, userId: null, keys: [] }` — the throttler genuinely runs **before** `AuthGuard`, so a `request.user.id` tracker would have read `undefined` every time and re-keyed the whole app into one bucket **while looking fixed**. Four tiers instead: `request.user.id` (kept and documented as unreachable today, so it wins automatically if the order ever changes), then the token's `sub` then `oid` read **unverified and only to pick a bucket**, then the dev headers, then IP. They rejected hashing the whole token for the reason the card gave — reissued on refresh, so it hands one person a fresh allowance hourly — and there is a test for that. ✅ **The dev-header tier is gated on the same predicate `AuthGuard.shouldAllowInsecureHeaders()` uses**, which I had not thought of: bucketing on `x-user-email` in production would let anyone name a header and spend a chosen victim's budget. **Step 3:** only `GET /api/health` exempted, chosen by reading the live `siteConfig.healthCheckPath`; `/api/health/ready` deliberately left limited because it is `@Public()`, fans out to every integration and would be a free amplifier. **Step 4:** ten count requests became **one** — the sidebar now shares `App.tsx`'s existing query key, so React Query serves both from a single request. The conclusion held; the reasoning did not.** **(a) It is NOT one bucket for the whole application — it is one per ROUTE HANDLER.** `@nestjs/throttler`'s default `generateKey` is `sha256(ClassName-handlerName-throttlerName-tracker)` (`throttler.guard.js:148-151`), so the handler is part of the key. Combined with the constant IP that means **one bucket per endpoint, shared by every user** — bad, but not what I wrote. **Their confirming detail is the good one: all 288 refusals were `/api/tickets`, which is exactly what per-handler keying predicts and a single global bucket does not.** **(b) `getTracker!` and `generateKey!` were NOT assertions on something never set.** `onModuleInit` does `commonOptions.getTracker ??= this.getTracker.bind(this)` (`throttler.guard.js:57-58`), so both are set — to the guard's own defaults. I called them a lie; they were correct. **(c) My instructed fix would have covered half the routes.** I said to add a `getTracker` to the `ThrottlerModule` config. The array element is read by the **default** path (`:85`) but the guard's `webhook`/`highWrite` branch passes `commonOptions.getTracker` directly — **so the intake webhook and every high-write route would have stayed on `req.ip`.** Overriding the guard's **method** fixes both paths. **They found this; my document would have shipped a half-fix that tested green.** ✅ **What did hold:** the throttle was keyed on `req.ip`, there is no `trust proxy`, every production request logs the same `remoteAddress: 169.254.131.5`, and **the trap I flagged in step 2 was real behaviour rather than a hypothetical** — their probe returned `{ hasUser: false, userId: null, keys: [] }`, so the throttler genuinely runs before `AuthGuard` and a `request.user.id` tracker would have re-keyed everything into one bucket while looking fixed. **✅ The other session's mechanism is right:** the `ThrottlerModule` config supplies `name`, `ttl`, `limit` and `setHeaders` and **no `getTracker`** — so `route-throttler.guard.ts:55` forwards `this.commonOptions.getTracker!`, a non-null assertion on something that was never set, and the library default applies: **`req.ip`.** There is **no `trust proxy`** anywhere. **⚠️ WORSE THAN REPORTED, FIRST AXIS.** They wrote that everyone behind the corporate NAT would share one allowance. **It is not per-NAT — it is per-application.** Without `trust proxy`, `req.ip` is the immediate peer, which on App Service is the platform ingress. **Every one of 1,640 production requests logs the same `remoteAddress: 169.254.131.5`** — a link-local address. **So all users, everywhere, plus the platform's own health probes, share a single 120-per-minute budget.** That also means **the IP fallback is worthless**: keying by user is not the better option, it is the only one that does anything. **⚠️ WORSE THAN REPORTED, SECOND AXIS.** Their counts were taken mid-burst. Re-measured from a fresh download the same day: **1,628 requests to `/api/tickets` (they saw 595), 956 of them `pageSize=1` count queries (449), 268 presence pings (109), and 288 × 429 (20).** **Every one of the 288 refusals was `/api/tickets`.** The volume is still climbing, which points at a feedback loop — a refused count query being refired. **⚠️ `/api/health` is NOT throttle-exempt**, so the platform's own probe — which fires about once a minute and made up nearly all traffic in a quiet window — spends from the same bucket. **Not caused by any recent deploy**, and the other session was right to say so: the throttle default and the count-per-badge design both predate today, and **card 1.65's fix is working correctly** — it suppresses *aborts*, while a 429 is a real failure that should be visible. **Same shape as card 1.20**, where idempotency was keyed by `ip:port` from `X-Forwarded-For`. `strip-port.util.ts` exists because of it. **The throttler never got the same treatment.** **Order to fix, planner's revision of theirs:** **(1) Raise `RATE_LIMIT_LIMIT` now** — it is an app setting, needs no deploy, and is the only relief available today. **(2) Add a `getTracker` keyed on the authenticated user id**, since the IP fallback is inert. **(3) Exempt `/api/health`.** **(4) Replace the ~11 parallel `pageSize=1` calls with one batched count endpoint** — the master plan predicted this under card 1.53 (*"fourteen filtered counts per sidebar load"*) and card 1.67 counted it again. **(1) is minutes; (2)–(4) are S–M.** |

The **list uses 4h** and `completedAt IS NULL`. **`atRisk` uses `SLA_AT_RISK_THRESHOLD_MINUTES`, which defaults to 120 — 2h — and is unset in production**, and it **ignores `completedAt`.** `tickets.service.ts:846` already carries a comment noting the mismatch. **Planner's recommendation, three parts:** use the **configurable threshold** as the single definition; **respect `completedAt IS NULL`**, because a completed ticket cannot breach; and ⚠️ **derive the label from the setting instead of hard-coding it.** That last part is the one that matters — **a hard-coded “1h” beside a configurable threshold will drift again the moment anyone changes the setting, and it has already drifted twice.** Fixing the number without fixing the label just resets the clock. **Nothing is broken today:** each badge kept its own field, so no number moved. `DashboardPage` has shown the narrower figure in both cases for months, so **the dashboard and the sidebar have been disagreeing in production and nobody noticed** — which is its own argument for picking one. **XS once decided.** |

| 1.74 Outlook splits one ticket into two conversations | **✅ GREEN — CONFIRMED BY THE OWNER IN OUTLOOK, 2026-09-11.** The fix is live in production (`cc0ce09`) and **Outlook now shows one conversation instead of two.** **This was the last item in Phase 1 that no test could close** — it needed a real mail client, and the owner ran it. **What it also closes:** the threading workstream is now proven end to end against a real inbox rather than against headers alone. Card 1.33 was never at fault — live Internet headers showed both messages on one `<ticket.…>` root with the second referencing the first, so any compliant client already threaded them. **Outlook is not one:** it groups on `ConversationTopic` and `ConversationIndex`. `Thread-Topic` had been the **tagged** subject, so every message we sent carried `… [PA_20260910_381]` while the requester's own mail carried the untagged one — different topic, different conversation, permanently. It now sends `canonicalSubject`; the Subject line keeps its tag. `Thread-Index` was absent entirely, so Exchange had to infer a conversation, and the tenant's security gateway re-injects each message separately, which is exactly where inference fails. ⚠️ **The lesson, recorded because it recurred within a day:** this commit (`239646c`) shipped mislabelled as card 1.66 with no board row, and was caught only because the planner read the deploy set rather than the board. **Card 1.75 then did the same thing.** Two real production fixes in two days arrived with no card — both found by the owner using the product, not by the process. | — no handoff; built directly | **The card exists to hold a proof nobody was booked to do.** A requester got the acknowledgement and the agent's reply as two separate Outlook conversations. ✅ **Our RFC 5322 threading was innocent and was checked** — live Internet headers from delivered mail show both messages on the same `<ticket.…>` root with the second referencing the first, so card 1.33 works and any compliant client threads them. **Outlook is not one:** it groups on `ConversationTopic` and `ConversationIndex`. `Thread-Topic` was the **tagged** subject — Outlook strips `RE:`/`FW:` when deriving a topic but not a bracketed suffix, so every message we sent carried `… [PA_20260910_381]` while the requester's own mail carried the untagged one. Different topic, different conversation, permanently. It now sends `TicketEmailThread.canonicalSubject`; the Subject line keeps its tag. `Thread-Index` was absent, so Exchange had to infer a conversation — and the tenant's security gateway re-injects each message separately, which is where inference fails. `buildThreadIndex` derives a stable 22-byte root block from the same `replyToken` the root message-id already uses: no new column, no second piece of state to drift. Every message gets the ROOT block, so ordering falls back to Date, which is the right order anyway. Both headers are optional all the way down, so outbox rows queued before it shipped still send. ✅ **Covered by the 2026-09-11 green run** (794/650/271) since it is an ancestor of `HEAD`; its own commit recorded api tsc 0, unit 621/62. ⚠️ **But no test can close this card — only a mailbox can.** **OUTSTANDING: send two messages on one ticket after the deploy and confirm Outlook shows ONE conversation.** Booked into `prompts/2026-09-11-deploy-eleven-commits.md` §6. |

The thirteenth instance of one-rule-two-places, closed. | `prompts/2026-09-15-nine-card-batch.md` | ✅ **VERIFIED TRUE.** `rule-engine.service.ts:1138` is `private addHours(date, hours) { return new Date(date.getTime() + hours*60*60*1000); }` — **raw milliseconds** — and it is used at `:666`, `:672`, `:675` and `:679` to recompute `firstResponseDueAt` and `dueAt` when a rule or macro changes priority. **The same operation done through `POST /tickets/bulk/priority` uses `TicketSlaCalculationService` with `businessHoursOnly`.** So the same priority change produces two different deadlines depending on which path made it, and the error compounds on every subsequent change because the wrong value is written back to `SlaInstance`. **This is the one-rule-two-places shape again — the thirteenth instance.** Card 1.72 collapsed “finished” into one definition; this is the same job for “when is it due”. **Fix: call the SLA calculator with the policy flags and share one derivation with `bulkPriority`.** **Regression test: a macro and a bulk priority change on identical Friday-afternoon tickets must produce equal `dueAt`** — Friday afternoon because that is when business-hours and wall-clock diverge most. |
| 1.83 ⚠️ Internal-note attachments are visible to the requester | **GREEN** — verified 2026-09-15, planner re-ran everything on a clean tree: 765 unit / 78, 385 web / 56, **963 integration + 1 skipped / 96 of 97, 0 failures, 0 environment markers**, migrations **67**, api tsc 0. Commit `10bf9c0`. ⚠️ **THE CARD SAID “POPULATE `messageId` ON UPLOAD” AND THAT IS IMPOSSIBLE — the implementer found it by building.** An attachment is uploaded to the TICKET and gets its id **before any message exists**; the editor pastes that id into the body afterwards. **Message creation is the first moment the link is knowable**, so that is where it is written, reusing `inlineAttachmentIds` and scoped to the ticket. ✅ **ONE RULE, NOT TWO.** `canSeeInternalMessages` is lifted **verbatim** out of `listMessages` into a leaf helper (`common/can-see-internal-messages.util.ts`) and `listMessages` now calls it too — **planner-verified: three callers, `tickets.service.ts:1441`, `:1567`, `ticket-attachment.service.ts:282`, and no fourth copy anywhere.** ✅ **Planner-verified there is exactly ONE requester-visible listing site and ONE download site**, both gated; the only other `attachment.findMany` is the redaction path, agent-only and not a listing. ✅ **The safe direction is preserved:** `messageId: null` and `PUBLIC` stay visible, so **every file uploaded before today still shows**. ⚠️ **The rule is relationship, not rank** — the requester is excluded whatever their role, because Payroll is the only department operationally taking tickets, so a payroll lead files her own pay problem in her own queue. A test covers exactly that. ⚠️ **403 and not 404 on refusal**, deliberately: the person can see the ticket, so its existence is not the secret — only this file is. | `prompts/2026-09-15-attachments-and-inbound-batch.md` | ✅ **VERIFIED TRUE at the schema level:** `model Attachment` has `ticketId` and **no `messageId`** — attachments belong to a ticket, not to a message. `tickets.service.ts:1352-1356` lists every attachment on the ticket, and download is gated on `canViewTicket` alone. **So a screenshot pasted into an INTERNAL note is listed to the requester with its filename, size and uploader** — and the audit confirmed at runtime that an agent's upload was downloadable by the requester (200), while non-participants got 403 and anonymous got 401. ⚠️ **On a healthcare desk the filename alone can be the disclosure** — the wrong patient's chart, a colleague's payslip. **Today the production scan gate blocks the download itself, so the live leak is the listing; the download leak arms the moment card 0.7's scanner is wired or `ATTACHMENT_SCAN_ENABLED=false` is set.** **That makes this a prerequisite for card 0.7, not a parallel task.** **Fix: `Attachment.messageId`, then exclude INTERNAL-message attachments from both the requester's listing and download.** |

But the script it loads (`email-action-page.util.ts:125`) calls `fetch(window.location.pathname, { method: 'POST' })` **immediately on load, with no click and no confirmation.** **So anything that opens the link AND executes JavaScript performs the action** — which is precisely what Defender for Office 365 Safe Links and similar gateways do when they detonate a URL. ⚠️ **This tenant demonstrably HAS such a gateway** — card 1.74 exists because it re-injects each message separately. ✅ **AND IT HAS NOT HAPPENED YET — the planner checked production rather than assuming.** Only **4** `TICKET_ACTION_FROM_EMAIL` events exist, on two tickets; the busiest ticket's three events span **21 hours 25 minutes**, which is a person, not a burst. **A detonating scanner would follow all SEVEN links in one go and produce seconds-apart events on one ticket. Nothing like that is in the data.** **Why it has not fired: pilot mode.** Emails only reach the test recipients today. **So the risk arrives the moment card 1.67③ clears `EMAIL_TEST_RECIPIENTS`** — which makes this a gate on that decision, exactly as card 1.83 gates card 0.7. **Fix: require a real interaction** — a button on the page rather than an automatic POST. Teams, routing, SLA, KB and tags now write audit rows through **one shared `AdminAuditService.record()`**. ✅ **And the error handling is right, which the planner checked: it RETHROWS inside a caller's transaction** — so a failed audit row rolls the change back with it — **and logs outside one**, where the change has already committed and there is nothing to undo. ⚠️ **The READ side is not right, and that is now card 1.104.** | `prompts/2026-09-15-nine-card-batch.md` | ✅ **VERIFIED TRUE by counting.** Of the privileged areas, only **`users` and `automation`** contain any audit write at all. **`teams`, `routing`, `slas`, `kb` and `tags` contain none** — zero files referencing the admin-audit service. **So changing a team, rewriting a routing rule, editing an SLA policy, publishing or deleting a knowledge-base article and managing tags all happen silently.** On a healthcare desk the question *“who changed the routing the week those tickets went to the wrong team”* currently has no answer. **Card 0.3's migration guard and card 1.21's operations console both exist because this project values being able to answer that afterwards** — this is the same instinct applied to configuration. **Fix: one shared admin-audit helper called from each of the five, not five implementations** — the audit's own recommendation and the right one, given that one-rule-many-places is the shape behind thirteen cards here. **Scope it: the write must not be able to fail silently** (the audit notes existing audit writes are swallowed). |
| 1.96 Ticket detail hands back more than it needs to | **GREEN** — verified 2026-09-15, planner re-ran everything on a clean tree: 754 unit / 75, 385 web / 56, **947 integration + 1 skipped / 93 of 94, 0 failures, 0 environment markers**, migrations 66, guard clean. ✅ **The implementer's one reported failure (`security.idempotency`) did NOT recur — contention, as they judged.** Commits `2bd289d` and `dd2a14a`. ⚠️ **The first fix was incomplete and the implementer's OWN test caught it:** narrowing `assignee` just moved the leak, because the agent is also a follower — so `entraObjectId`, department and location still reached every requester. **Only asserting on the whole serialised payload found it.** ✅ **Same lesson as the AI canary, and now twice in one month: assert on the payload, not the field.** | `prompts/2026-09-15-nine-card-batch.md` | ✅ **VERIFIED TRUE.** `tickets.service.ts` `getById` uses `include: { requester: true, assignee: true, assignedTeam: true, … }`. **In Prisma `include: { x: true }` returns every column of the related row**, so a requester opening their own ticket receives the agent's `entraObjectId`, `graphProfile` (the raw directory object), `department`, `location` and availability flags — plus the team's `isSensitive`, `confidenceThreshold`, `hiddenPresetIds`, `assignmentStrategy` and `lastAssignedUserId`. **This is not PHI and not an authorization hole** — the person is entitled to see the ticket. It is over-fetching that leaks internal configuration and directory attributes to whoever asks. **Fix: replace the `include: true` shorthands with explicit `select` blocks** naming the fields the UI actually renders. ⚠️ **Check the web app's types first** — the SPA may be reading fields nobody realised were arriving, and narrowing the payload without checking would break a screen. **The typecheck will tell you.** |
| 1.97 ⚠️ `apiFetch` hands a caller somebody else's cancelled request | **GREEN** — verified 2026-09-15, planner re-ran everything on a clean tree: 754 unit / 75, 385 web / 56, **947 integration + 1 skipped / 93 of 94, 0 failures, 0 environment markers**, migrations 66, guard clean. ✅ **The implementer's one reported failure (`security.idempotency`) did NOT recur — contention, as they judged.** Commit `e0990b9`. **The cause behind two earlier bugs is gone:** joining a shared in-flight GET no longer inherits the first caller's cancellation. ⚠️ **Card 1.65's test pinned the OLD behaviour and had to be deliberately rewritten** — that was the trap in this card and it was handled. | `prompts/2026-09-15-nine-card-batch.md` | **The mechanism, pinned at `client.ts:805-808`:** `const inflight = apiGetInflight.get(cacheKey); if (inflight) return inflight;` — the second caller gets the **first caller's promise**, built with the **first caller's** `requestInit` and therefore the first caller's signal. **The second caller's signal is discarded entirely, and the second caller never issues a request.** **Two symptoms, both now seen in production code:** **① Card 1.65** — a caller that passes NO signal inherits another's abort and shows *“Unable to load”* for a request that was never in trouble. Fixed with an `isAbortError` guard. **② Card 2.7** — a caller that DOES pass a signal gets a promise already rejecting, so **it never loads at all and shows nothing**. The banner sat empty on every route while `curl` returned the data correctly. Fixed with a `cancelled` flag and no `AbortController`. ⚠️ **So there are now TWO different workarounds in the codebase for ONE surprising behaviour, and neither fixes it** — which is the one-rule-two-places shape, at the layer underneath the rule. **The next component that passes a signal to a shared GET will hit it again**, and card 1.65's own test file already documents the mechanism without preventing it. **The fix is a decision, not a patch.** Either **(a)** share only when neither caller passed a signal, **(b)** ref-count so the underlying request is aborted only when every joiner has abandoned it, or **(c)** stop sharing across callers with signals. **⚠️ Whichever is chosen, card 1.65's `shared-inflight-abort.test.ts` pins the CURRENT behaviour and will have to be rewritten deliberately** — it asserts that aborting one caller rejects the other, which is the very thing worth changing. |
| 1.98 ⚠️ Reactivating someone does not put them back to work | **GREEN** — verified 2026-09-14, planner re-ran everything: 683 unit / 69, 377 web / 55, **903 integration + 1 skipped / 89 of 90, 0 failures, 0 environment markers**, migrations still 64, no migration file in any diff. Commit `f5f4b62`. `USER_DEACTIVATED` already existed and carried only counts; it now carries ids, names, roles and the primary team, and `payload` is `Json?` so **no migration was needed** — the cheap path the card recommended, avoiding the roster-soft-delete blast radius near card 2.2's picker. | `prompts/2026-09-14-state-correctness-batch.md` | ✅ **VERIFIED.** `users.service.ts` deactivation deletes the user's `TeamMember` rows; `reactivate` (`:311`) writes **`{ isActive: true, deactivatedAt: null }` and nothing else.** **The roster rows are gone and unrecoverable** — so a reactivated agent signs in successfully and sees an empty queue, on no team, until somebody re-adds them by hand. ⚠️ **And nothing records which teams they were on**, so the owner has to remember. On a desk with multi-team leads that is a real chance of quietly reinstating somebody with less access than they had. ⚠️ **This is pre-existing and card 1.78 did not cause it — but 1.78 is why it now matters.** Before that fix a “deactivated” person kept working, so reactivation was never exercised; now deactivation is real and reactivation is the path back. **Three ways to fix it, cheapest first.** **(a)** Record the team ids on the deactivation event and offer them at reactivation — **no schema change, no risk to card 2.2's picker, and the planner's recommendation.** **(b)** Soft-delete the roster rows instead of hard-deleting — correct but it is a migration AND it touches every roster query, including the assignment picker, which is a lot of blast radius for this. **(c)** Say nothing and document that re-adding is manual — defensible only if reactivation is rare. **Whichever is chosen, the test is the same: deactivate a member of two teams, reactivate, and assert they can work** — not merely that they can sign in. |
| 1.99 ⚠️ A ticket filter must be spelled in three places or it silently vanishes | **GREEN** — verified 2026-09-15, planner re-ran everything on a clean tree: 754 unit / 75, 385 web / 56, **947 integration + 1 skipped / 93 of 94, 0 failures, 0 environment markers**, migrations 66, guard clean. ✅ **The implementer's one reported failure (`security.idempotency`) did NOT recur — contention, as they judged.** Commit `944f5d9`. A filter missing from any of the three lists now fails a test instead of vanishing. **The failure direction was the dangerous one** — a missing name did not narrow the list, it WIDENED it. | `prompts/2026-09-15-nine-card-batch.md` | **`useFilters.ts` carries THREE hand-maintained lists of the same filter names:** the URL parser (**22** `searchParams.get(…)` calls), the URL writer (**24** `params.set(…)` calls) and the API forwarder (`apiParams`, `:187-217`). **A filter missing from any one of them does not error — it disappears.** ⚠️ **AND IT FAILS IN THE DANGEROUS DIRECTION.** Card 1.88 pointed a saved view at `resolvedFrom`; the parser did not know the word, so the date window was not narrowed — **it was removed**, and the request went out as `statusGroup=resolved` with no window at all, listing every resolved ticket ever. **The badge said 4 and the list showed 5**, including the exact ticket the card existed to exclude. ⚠️ **No API test can catch this and card 1.88's implementer said so plainly: the endpoint was always right.** The bug lives entirely in the web layer between a correct API and the screen — **which is where the last THREE browser-only findings have been** (the announcements banner that never loaded, the wrong timeline renderer, and this). **The parser and writer counts already differ by two**, so at least one name is in one list and not the other today. **Finding out which is step one.** **Fix: derive all three from one declaration of the filter set** — name, parse, serialise, forward — so adding a filter is one edit. **A test that the three lists agree is the cheap version and would have caught 1.88's miss**; one declaration is the real fix. **Same family as card 1.97**: a web-layer helper whose obvious use silently does nothing. |

Closing a closed ticket is a no-op; **submitting a satisfaction score repeatedly is not, and CSAT is a number the desk is judged on.** **Fix needs storage, therefore a migration.** ⚠️ **65 is reserved for card 2.6 and 66 for card 1.83** — take 67, or whatever is genuinely free when this is scheduled. **Consume the token on first successful use and answer a replay with the same friendly page rather than an error**, since the person did nothing wrong. |
